● All done. Here's a summary of the new design:

  Stateful Variables + UNASSIGNED Sentinel

  Variables now hold values. Each Variable has a _value field, defaulting to the
   UNASSIGNED singleton. The runtime executor sets values per loop iteration;
  expressions evaluate by reading variable values directly.

  New API

  freq = Variable("freq")
  freq.value             # UNASSIGNED (singleton, falsy)
  freq.evaluate()        # UNASSIGNED
  freq.set_value(5e9)
  freq.evaluate()        # 5e9
  freq.reset()           # back to UNASSIGNED

  evaluate() takes no arguments. UNASSIGNED propagates through expressions:
  - BinaryOp.evaluate() returns UNASSIGNED if either operand is UNASSIGNED
  - UnaryOp.evaluate() returns UNASSIGNED if its operand is UNASSIGNED
  - Constant.evaluate() always returns its value

  resolve() helper

  resolve(int | float | Expression) returns a concrete number, or raises
  ValueError if any variable is unassigned. This is what waveforms and
  operations use to handle both literal and symbolic parameters transparently:

  from qprogram import resolve

  resolve(100)              # 100
  resolve(freq + 10)        # works if freq has a value
  resolve(freq + 10)        # ValueError: unassigned

  Waveforms

  All waveforms now build envelopes via resolve():

  amp = program.variable("amp")
  g = Gaussian(amplitude=amp, duration=40, num_sigmas=2.5)
  g.envelope()              # ValueError: amp is UNASSIGNED
  amp.set_value(0.7)
  g.envelope()              # works — peak ≈ 0.7
  amp.set_value(1.0)
  g.envelope()              # re-evaluates, peak ≈ 1.0

  The same waveform instance reflects whatever value is currently bound — no
  need to recreate it each iteration.

  Files updated

  - variable.py — full rewrite with UNASSIGNED, stateful Variable, resolve()
  - All 7 variable-aware waveforms (Square, Gaussian, GaussianDragCorrection,
  Ramp, FlatTop, SuddenNetZero, IQDrag) now use resolve()
  - __init__.py — exports UNASSIGNED and resolve

  Notion DSL spec

  - Section 3.1 (class table) updated: Variable description now mentions it
  holds a value
  - Sections 3.5-3.7 rewritten:
    - 3.5 — Variables hold values (set_value, value, reset); evaluate() takes no
   args; UNASSIGNED propagation
    - 3.6 — resolve() helper with examples (including Waveform usage)
    - 3.7 — variables() for free variable extraction (independent of binding
  state)

      81 +    """Raised by ``evaluate_or_raise()`` when an expression con
         +tains an unassigned variable.                                  
      82 +

         +xception-handling code.

● All works. Now update the Notion spec to replace the resolve() documentation

  Called Notion (ctrl+o to expand)

● All done. Summary of the change:

  Removed: resolve() free function (no more global helper to import).

  Added on Expression: evaluate_or_raise() -> int | float — calls evaluate(),
  returns the numeric result or raises UnassignedVariableError if any variable
  is UNASSIGNED.

  Added new exception: UnassignedVariableError(ValueError) with .expression and
  .free_variables attributes for debugging.

  Two evaluation modes available on every Expression:

  ┌─────────────────────┬───────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┐
  │       Method        │        Returns        │        Failure mode        │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ evaluate()          │ int | float |         │ Returns UNASSIGNED         │
  │                     │ _UnassignedType       │                            │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ evaluate_or_raise() │ int | float           │ Raises                     │
  │                     │                       │ UnassignedVariableError    │
  └─────────────────────┴───────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┘

  Waveform pattern (no imports needed beyond Expression):
  amplitude = self.amplitude.evaluate_or_raise() if isinstance(self.amplitude,
  Expression) else self.amplitude

  All 7 variable-aware waveforms updated. __init__.py exports
  UnassignedVariableError. Notion spec section 3.6 rewritten.

  ┌─────────────┬───────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┐

  ├─────────────┼───────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤

  The proposal is anonymous-review-friendly — no Qilimanjaro/EuroQCS/personal
  info in the abstract or description. Author info goes in the separate speaker
  fields on pretalx.

  Title

  QProgram: A Python DSL for Pulse-Level Quantum Programming

  Track

  Computational Tools and Scientific Python Infrastructure (the poster is framed
   around DSL design and Python tooling more than quantum physics).

  Angle / story

  The EuroSciPy audience is Python tooling people, not necessarily quantum
  physicists. The poster pitches QProgram as a case study in:
  - Building a typed DSL with modern Python (context managers, operator
  overloading, AST, mixins)
  - A vendor-extension mechanism that preserves IDE autocomplete
  - A custom text file format with a 300-line pure-Python recursive-descent
  parser
  - xarray as the natural result container for scientific data

  Quantum physics concepts are introduced briefly so the poster stands on its
  own for non-quantum attendees.

  The file includes 3 alternative titles, a checklist for submission, and
  full-text drafts of all fields ready to copy-paste into pretalx.

❯ Do you think an A1 poster is suffient to fit all the information you promise  
  in the proposal? Also, do you think we should focus on very technical Python  
  information or mix between technical details and why we made QProgram (the    
  problem it solves)                                                            

● Good questions. Honest answer: the description I drafted is over-scoped for an
   A1 poster. Let me break down both.

  Will it fit on A1?

  A1 is 594 × 841 mm — generous for an academic poster, but not infinite. A
  realistic A1 layout supports roughly:

  - 1 title bar + author/affiliation strip
  - 1 short motivation/problem section (~150 words + 1 diagram)
  - 1 "hero" code example (10–20 lines, must be readable from 1m away → ~14pt)
  - 3–4 design-decision panels (~80 words + 1 small code snippet each)
  - 1 takeaways/QR-code panel

  The proposal promises 5 design decisions × code samples + 5 reusable-tradeoff 
  bullets. That's roughly 10 code blocks. On A1 it would either feel cramped or
  force the font down to "you must lean in to read" territory. The proposal text
   is fine — that's for reviewers, who want to see depth — but for the actual
  artifact I'd cut to 3 design decisions + 2 tradeoffs.

  Strongest 3 to feature (broadest EuroSciPy appeal):

  1. Symbolic Expression AST — universally interesting; mirrors what compiler
  authors / sympy users / autograd folks do
  2. BusSchema + BusRef(str) — clever, memorable Python trick (typed accessors
  that are still strings); short to demo
  3. Vendor extensions via mixins — broadly reusable plugin pattern,
  multi-vendor composition with full IDE typing

  Drop from the poster (keep in talking points / handout):
  - .qp file format internals (mention in 1 sentence + show snippet)
  - xarray results (one-liner)
  - The two more philosophical tradeoffs (__getattr__ vs @property,
  dataclasses-vs-classes — these are great in conversation, not on the wall)

  Technical-only vs. mix problem + technical

  Mix, leaning slightly technical (~25% problem / 65% technical / 10% 
  takeaways).

  EuroSciPy audience is Python practitioners, mostly scientific computing — most
   will not know quantum computing. If you skip the "why" entirely, the poster
  looks like decontextualized Python tricks. If you over-explain quantum, you
  lose the people who'd actually engage with the DSL design.

  The hook the poster needs:

  ▎ "Quantum computers are programmed at the gate level (H, CNOT, RZ), but to 
  ▎ actually run one, every gate decomposes into precisely shaped microwave 
  ▎ pulses on real wires. Calibrating those pulses is where physics happens. 
  ▎ Tooling here is fragmented and vendor-locked. We built a Python DSL to fix 
  ▎ that — here's how."

  That's 4–5 sentences + maybe one small diagram (gate → pulses on
  drive/readout/flux lines). Then the rest of the wall is what's interesting 
  from a Python perspective, with quantum concepts introduced in passing as
  needed.

  Concrete A1 layout I'd propose

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TITLE                                                       │
  │  Author · Affiliation · Repo URL · QR code                   │
  ├──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Why pulse           │  HERO EXAMPLE                         │
  │  programming?        │  (Rabi or chevron — 15-line code     │
  │  · Gate level vs     │   block, big enough to read at 1m)   │
  │    pulse level       │                                       │
  │  · Vendor lock-in    │  + small diagram: program → compiler │
  │  · Why it's hard     │    → hardware                        │
  ├──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Design 1: Symbolic Expressions    │ Design 2: Bus Schema   │
  │  (Variable / Constant /            │ (str subclass +        │
  │   BinaryOp / UnaryOp tree)         │  typed accessors)      │
  │  + 5-line code + tiny tree diagram │ + 5-line code         │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────┤
  │  Design 3: Vendor extensions (mixins)                        │
  │  + multi-vendor composition example                         │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Takeaways · References · QR to repo + .qp file format      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Roughly 7 panels. Comfortable on A1 at 18–24pt body / 32pt section heads.

  Two suggestions for the proposal text

  1. Tighten the description so the reviewers' expectations match what fits on
  the poster. I'd:
    - Reduce the "Selected design decisions" from 5 to 3 (matching the actual
  poster), and move the other 2 to a "we'll also touch on…" sentence
    - Cut the 5 reusable tradeoffs to 2 (the str-subclassing one and the mixin
  one — both broadly applicable)
  2. Add one explicit "what fits on the poster" sentence so reviewers don't
  expect a textbook. Something like:

  ▎ "The poster focuses on three design decisions in detail (symbolic 
  expressions, typed bus references, mixin-based vendor extensions), with the 
  remaining design choices summarized at the bottom and discussed verbally 
  during the session."

  Want me to apply those tightenings to the description in the file?

   the same as v1.

※ recap: Goal: submit a poster proposal to EuroSciPy 2026 about QProgram, the pulse-programming DSL. Two drafts now exist in .tmp/ — a technical-Python framing (v1) and a big-picture framing (v2). Next: pick which version to submit. (disable recaps in /config)

❯ What do you think of the following approach? You can edit as you see fit and  
  make it into a v3 document. "Quantum experiments are often written close to   
  the hardware: the scientific idea, timing assumptions, calibration choices,   
  and vendor-specific control instructions all live in the same layer. This     
  makes experiments harder to share, reproduce, review, and move between        
  setups.                                                                       
                                                                                
  QProgram is a Python DSL for describing pulse-level quantum experiments at    
  the level of experimental intent. Instead of tying an experiment directly to  
  one control stack, QProgram lets users express what should happen, then defer 
   hardware-specific details to backend extensions and compilation layers. The  
  same experiment description can be inspected, serialized, remapped to a       
  different setup, or extended with vendor-specific capabilities when needed.   
                                                                                
  This poster presents why this abstraction matters for quantum research        
  workflows, how QProgram separates portable experiment logic from              
  device-specific operations, and how this design can support reproducibility,  
  collaboration, and long-term maintainability in scientific Python             
  quantum-control software." and "Quantum experiments are software artifacts as 
   much as laboratory procedures. A pulse-level experiment usually encodes the  
  scientific question, the measurement protocol, calibration assumptions,       
  hardware channels, waveform choices, acquisition settings, synchronization    
  details, and vendor-specific control features. When all of this is written    
  directly against a single hardware API, the resulting code can work well in   
  one lab while becoming difficult to reuse, compare, or reproduce elsewhere.   
                                                                                
  QProgram is designed around a simple idea: the experiment should be           
  represented independently from the control system that eventually runs it.    
  Researchers should be able to describe the intent of an experiment in Python, 
   keep that description inspectable and serializable, and only later bind it   
  to concrete hardware details.                                                 
                                                                                
  This separation matters because quantum hardware changes quickly. A lab may   
  upgrade control electronics, compare vendors, maintain several device         
  generations, or run similar experiments across different cryostats. Without   
  an abstraction layer, each change can force experiment code to be rewritten.  
  With QProgram, the stable part of the workflow is the experiment description, 
   while setup-specific details can be handled through mappings and backend     
  extensions.                                                                   
                                                                                
  The poster will explain how QProgram achieves this in practice:               
                                                                                
  - A user writes a quantum experiment in Python using a compact                
  pulse-programming interface.                                                  
  - The experiment is stored as a structured, inspectable representation rather 
   than as an opaque script.                                                    
  - Hardware names and calibrated pulse definitions can be attached or remapped 
   separately from the experiment logic.                                        
  - The program can be serialized to a readable .qp format for review, storage, 
   or exchange.                                                                 
  - Vendor-specific features are added through extension namespaces, so backend 
   capabilities remain available without making the core experiment description 
   vendor-dependent.                                                            
                                                                                
  For example, a generic experiment can express “drive the qubit, measure the   
  response, repeat over a sweep” without committing the whole program to one    
  hardware vendor. If a particular backend exposes extra capabilities, those    
  can be accessed through an extension while preserving the same overall        
  programming model.                                                            
                                                                                
  The poster will focus on the broader scientific software lesson: abstractions 
   are useful when they preserve the language of the experiment while isolating 
   the parts that change across instruments, calibrations, and vendors.         
  QProgram applies this principle to pulse-level quantum programming, aiming to 
   make quantum experiments easier to reproduce, port, and maintain in          
  Python-based research workflows."                                             

● I really like that framing — it speaks to EuroSciPy's audience much more
  directly. The "scientific software lesson" angle (preserving the language of
  the experiment, isolating what changes) is exactly what makes this poster
  matter to non-quantum attendees.

  Let me create v3, polishing the prose lightly and adding small structural
  elements (a code example, a "what fits on the poster" panel) without changing
  your core framing.

  What changed and why

  Second sentence (the one you flagged):
  - Old: "His work spans SDKs, simulators, runtimes, pulse-level programming, 
  hardware-aware execution, and HPC integration, with a strong focus on usable 
  abstractions and developer experience." — six nouns in a list, then a generic
  closing clause.
  - New: "His work centres on the abstractions that make quantum hardware 
  programmable across the full stack — from pulse-level control and runtime 
  systems through SDKs and simulators, up to integration with classical HPC." —
  leads with the thesis (designing the abstractions that make quantum hardware
  programmable), then walks the stack from bottom (pulse-level control) to top
  (HPC integration). The list is still there, but it's now ordered as a story
  instead of an inventory.
  - "Novel" is implicit in "designs the abstractions" — using "novel" explicitly
   often reads weaker than letting "designs" do the work, but happy to put it
  back if you want it called out.

  Other small touches (optional):
  - Last sentence — "extensive experience in training, technical consulting,
  public speaking, and mentoring" → "a regular speaker, trainer, and mentor".
  Same content, half the words, sounds active rather than résumé-padded.
  ("Technical consulting" is dropped; tell me if you want it kept.)

  Three alternative second sentences to choose from

  If you'd prefer a different angle:

  Option A — current recommendation, "full stack" framing:

  ▎ His work centres on the abstractions that make quantum hardware programmable
  ▎  across the full stack — from pulse-level control and runtime systems 
  ▎ through SDKs and simulators, up to integration with classical HPC.

  Option B — emphasises the design/research dimension:

  ▎ His work focuses on designing novel abstractions that bridge quantum 
  ▎ hardware and the scientists who use it — across pulse-level control, runtime
  ▎  systems, SDKs and simulators, and integration with classical HPC.

  Option C — emphasises the user/developer outcome:

  ▎ His work designs the abstractions that turn experimental quantum hardware 
  ▎ into a programmable platform — from pulse-level control, through SDKs, 
  ▎ simulators, and runtimes, to integration with classical HPC.

  I'd pick A for a conference bio (compact, walks the stack, ends on the
  strongest hook for EuroSciPy reviewers — HPC integration is right in their
  tribe), but B and C work well in different contexts (B for academic/research
  bios; C for product/engineering audiences).

❯ Alternatives to "centers on"                                                  

● A few categories of alternatives, with brief commentary on the feel of each:

  Neutral / direct

  - focuses on — the most common substitute, neutral and safe.
  - is built around — slightly more concrete; "the work is constructed around
  X".
  - is grounded in — research-leaning, suggests rigour and foundations.

  "X is the organising theme"

  These keep the same idea — abstractions are the through-line — but with a
  different metaphor:

  - revolves around — everything orbits the central concern. Fits well when you
  want abstractions framed as the unifying axis.
  - is anchored in / anchored around — slightly more academic; suggests a stable
   foundation that everything else is moored to.
  - is organised around — clinical/structural; useful in a more formal/CV
  register.

  Active-voice (changes the sentence structure)

  These are the strongest options if you want the bio to read more like you 
  doing things than your work having a topic:

  - I design novel abstractions and paradigms that make quantum hardware 
  programmable… — most active, most confident, my top pick.
  - I build novel abstractions and paradigms that… — same energy, slightly more
  concrete/engineering.
  - I work on novel abstractions and paradigms that… — softer, but more humble;
  good if "design" feels too bold.

  My recommendations, in order

  1. I design — for the first-person version. Strong, confident, leads with
  action. "I design novel abstractions and paradigms that make quantum hardware 
  programmable across the full stack — from…"
  2. focuses on — for the third-person version. "His work focuses on novel 
  abstractions…" — cleaner than "centers on" without changing the rhythm.
  3. revolves around — if you want to keep "His/My work" as the subject but with
   more flavour.

  Want me to update the file to use I design in first person and His work 
  focuses on in third person?

❯ Is it ok to use the word "also" as we do for the Phd?                         
● Yes, it's perfectly fine — "also" is a natural and very common bio connective.
   But there's a case for tightening:

  How it reads

  ▎ He is also pursuing a PhD in quantum control…

  "Also" signals "in addition to the work just described in paragraph 1, he is
  doing this." That's a useful bridge between the engineering paragraph and the
  research paragraph.

  Mild concerns (style-only, not grammar)

  - Filler-y feel. Bios are usually dense; "also" can read as a soft transition
  that doesn't add information.
  - Slightly diminishing. "He is also doing X" can subtly position X as
  secondary — fine when the first item is clearly the headline (in your case,
  the Tech Lead role), but worth noticing if you'd rather the PhD stand on its
  own.
  - Paired with "Alongside" later. The same paragraph already says "Alongside 
  his engineering and research work…" — two soft connectives in one paragraph
  can feel slightly over-engineered, though they're doing different work
  (paragraph-bridge vs sentence-bridge), so it's not really a problem.

  Alternatives, from least to most change

  1. Keep as-is — "He is also pursuing a PhD…" — totally fine.
  2. Drop "also" — "He is pursuing a PhD in quantum control…" — slightly more
  confident; treats the PhD as its own headline.
  3. More deliberate transition — "In parallel, he is pursuing a PhD…" or
  "Concurrently, he is pursuing a PhD…" — formal/academic register.
  4. Restructure as one long sentence — "He is pursuing a PhD in quantum control
   alongside his engineering work, focused on…" — saves a sentence but loses the
   clean paragraph break.

  My recommendation

  I'd drop "also" in both versions. It tightens the prose, removes the implicit
  "secondary" framing, and lets the PhD paragraph stand as its own beat:

  ▎ He is pursuing a PhD in quantum control, focused on pulse optimization and 
  ▎ gate design for high-fidelity operation of superconducting qubits.

  ▎ I'm pursuing a PhD in quantum control, focused on pulse optimization and 
  ▎ gate design for high-fidelity operation of superconducting qubits.

  Want me to apply this to the file?

❯ Back to QProgram design (DSL + .qp file + notion pages). Should we add a      
  version to the vendor extensions as well?                                     

● Yes — and the symmetry argument alone is compelling: the file already declares
   its language version (#!qp 1.0), so it's odd for vendor dependencies to be
  unversioned. But there are stronger reasons too.

  Why add versions

  Vendor extensions evolve independently from the core. qprogram-qblox could be
  at 2.3 while qprogram is at 1.0. They ship on separate cadences, separate
  teams, separate breaking changes. Without a version, a .qp file is a
  dependency declaration with no version constraint — equivalent to
  requirements.txt listing only package names.

  Real failure modes the version would catch:
  - A file uses qblox.measure_reset, but the receiver's qprogram-qblox is too
  old to know that operation. Without versions, the error is generic: "unknown 
  operation qblox.measure_reset". With versions, it's actionable: "this file 
  requires qprogram-qblox >= 1.5; you have 1.2".
  - An operation's signature changes between major versions (e.g. a parameter
  renamed). The file loads, runs silently with the wrong arguments, and produces
   wrong results. With versions, the parser refuses to load.
  - Vendor adds a new bus type, new channel semantics, or new metadata. A file
  written against the new version can't be safely interpreted by the old one. A
  version check rejects it cleanly.

  Reproducibility is the QProgram pitch. The framing in the EuroSciPy proposal
  is "one team writes an experiment, another team loads it on different 
  hardware". A versionless dependency declaration undermines that — the two
  teams may have different vendor extensions installed, and silently get
  different behaviour.

  Design options

  require qblox                  # A: any version (current)
  require qblox 1.0              # B: a single version number
  require qblox >=1.0,<2.0       # C: PEP 440 specifier

  My recommendation: B, with semver-style "compatible with X.Y" semantics.
  Specifically:

  - require qblox 1.0 means "any 1.x version of qprogram-qblox" (i.e.
  >=1.0,<2.0).
  - Matches the #!qp 1.0 style — one number, not a constraint expression.
  - Vendor commits to backward compatibility within a major version (the
  convention qprogram already implicitly relies on with its own 1.0 header).
  - Bare require qblox (no version) is allowed for backward compatibility but
  emits a warning and is discouraged in serialised output.
  - The writer always includes the installed vendor version when serialising, so
   round-tripping a file pins it.

  PEP 440 specifiers (option C) feel like overkill for a .qp file format. The
  point of .qp is human-readable simplicity; introducing a constraint
  mini-language inside it adds parser complexity for a use case that's
  adequately served by "compatible with N.M". If a user genuinely needs tighter
  pinning, they can pin via pip install qprogram-qblox==1.2.3 in their
  environment.

  How the parser uses it

  1. Parser reads require qblox 1.0.
  2. Looks up the registered vendor qblox (this still must be registered at
  import time, as today).
  3. Reads the installed extension's version from qprogram_qblox.__version__ (a
  small contract on the extension package).
  4. Checks compatibility:
    - Major versions must match: file says 1.x, installed must be 1.x.
    - Installed minor must be >= file's minor.
  5. On mismatch: clear error before parsing the body. "qprogram-qblox 1.5 or 
  compatible required; found 1.2" / "qprogram-qblox 2.0 incompatible with file 
  written for 1.0".

  Implementation surface

  Small. Concrete changes:

  ┌───────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────┐
  │             Location              │               Change                │
  ├───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ qprogram_qblox/__init__.py        │ Expose __version__ = "1.0.0"        │
  ├───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                   │ Vendor registration accepts an      │
  │ qprogram.serialization.registry   │ optional version:                   │
  │                                   │ register_vendor("qblox",            │
  │                                   │ QbloxNamespace, version="1.0")      │
  ├───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                   │ Tokenises require <vendor>          │
  │ Parser                            │ [<version>], runs compatibility     │
  │                                   │ check after import-time             │
  │                                   │ registration is verified            │
  ├───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                   │ Emits the installed vendor version  │
  │ Writer                            │ automatically; backward-compat:     │
  │                                   │ don't emit if vendor didn't         │
  │                                   │ register one                        │
  ├───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Notion DSL spec (Section 5.4)     │ Document the version field          │
  ├───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Notion .qp spec (Section 2.2      │ Add the version syntax              │
  │ "Require Declarations")           │                                     │
  ├───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Grammar (Section 7)               │ require := "require" IDENT          │
  │                                   │ [VERSION]                           │
  └───────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┘

  Open question worth flagging

  What about the core? Today the file header is #!qp 1.0, which is the language 
  version — what operations, blocks, syntax are defined. That's the right thing
  to version. We shouldn't conflate it with the implementation version of
  qprogram itself (the Python package). Vendors are similar: require qblox 1.0
  is the vendor extension protocol version (the operations they expose), not the
   Python package version. The two coincide today because both are at 1.0, but
  the spec should be careful to say "this is the version of the vendor's
  published operation set, not the package version on PyPI". Otherwise we end up
   with a .qp file that fails because the Python package version bumped for an
  unrelated bugfix.

  ---
  Want me to:

  1. Implement this in the code (qprogram registry + parser/writer +
  qprogram-qblox exposing __version__)?
  2. Update both Notion pages (DSL spec Section 5.4, .qp format spec Section 2.2
   + grammar)?

  If yes to both, I'd do them in that order — code first so I can verify the
  design works end-to-end, then the spec.

      392 +def _parse_major_minor(version: str) -> tuple[int, int]:      
      393 +    """Parse the leading ``major.minor`` of a version string. 
      394 +                                                              
      395 +    Accepts ``"0.1"``, ``"1.0.3"``, etc. Raises ``ValueError``
          + if the format                                                
      396 +    is not recognised. Patch and trailing components are ignor
          +ed.                                                           
      397 +    """                                                       
      398 +    parts = version.split(".")                                
      399 +    if len(parts) < 2:                                        
      400 +        msg = f"version {version!r} must have at least major.m
          +inor"                                                         
      401 +        raise ValueError(msg)                                 
      402 +    try:                                                      
      403 +        return int(parts[0]), int(parts[1])                   
      404 +    except ValueError as e:                                   
      405 +        msg = f"version {version!r} has non-integer major/mino
          +r components"                                                 
      406 +        raise ValueError(msg) from e

❯ There is the following serious bug: The serialization / deserialization to    
  .qp file, stores just the label of the variable. But two or more variables    
  may have same label. That means that there is no distinguish which variable   
  is used where. E.g. "program = qp.QProgram(label="qubit_spectroscopy",        
  description="Sweep drive frequency to find qubit resonance")                  
                                                                                
  freq = program.variable("freq")                                               
  freq2 = program.variable("freq")                                              
                                                                                
  with program.average(shots=1000), program.for_loop(freq, start=4.5e9,         
  stop=5.5e9, step=1e6):                                                        
      with program.for_loop(freq2, 100e6, 200e6, 10e6):                         
          # Set drive frequency for this iteration                              
          program.set_frequency(q[0].drive, freq)                               
          program.set_frequency(q[0].readout, freq2)                            
                                                                                
          # Play spectroscopy pulse on drive (IQ bus — validated)               
          program.play(q[0].drive, spec_pulse)                                  
          program.sync()                                                        
                                                                                
          # Measure on readout (has ADC — validated)                            
          program.measure(q[0].readout, readout_pulse, readout_weights)" is     
  saved to .qp as "#!QProgram 1.0                                               
                                                                                
  metadata:                                                                     
    label: "qubit_spectroscopy"                                                 
    description: "Sweep drive frequency to find qubit resonance"                
                                                                                
  body:                                                                         
    var freq                                                                    
    var freq                                                                    
                                                                                
    average 1000:                                                               
      for freq in range(4500000000.0, 5500000000.0, 1000000.0):                 
        for freq in range(100000000.0, 200000000.0, 10000000.0):                
          set_frequency "q0/drive" freq                                         
          set_frequency "q0/readout" freq                                       
          play "q0/drive" IQPair(I=Square(amplitude=0.05, duration=10000),      
  Q=Square(amplitude=0.0, duration=10000))                                      
          sync                                                                  
          measure "q0/readout" IQPair(I=Square(amplitude=0.5, duration=2000),   
  Q=Square(amplitude=0.0, duration=2000)) IQPair(I=Square(amplitude=1.0,        
  duration=2000), Q=Square(amplitude=1.0, duration=2000))                       
  " and there is no distinquish between the two variables freq and freq. Also   
  labels can be strings with spaces etc. so the label in .qp file should be     
  contained within quotes (as is done in buses for example)

● Now let me rewrite the writer cleanly as a class with per-program state:

● Write(qprogram/src/qprogram/serialization/writer.py)
  ⎿  Added 271 lines, removed 166 lines
       1 +"""Serializer for the ``.qp`` file format.                     
       2 +                                                               
       3 +Implemented as a small ``_Writer`` class so per-program state —
         + most importantly                                              
       4 +the variable identifier map — can be carried as instance state 
         +without threading                                              
       5 +it through every helper.                                       
       6 +                                                               
       7 +Why a variable identifier map: variables in the AST are identit
         +y-based, but the                                               
       8 +``.qp`` file references them by name. Two ``Variable`` instance
         +s created with                                                 
       9 +the same label (e.g. ``program.variable("freq")`` twice) must b
         +e distinguished                                                
      10 +in the file or load-time identity is lost. The writer assigns e
         +ach variable a                                                 
      11 +unique identifier — derived from its label, disambiguated by su
         +ffix when                                                      
      12 +needed — and emits the human-readable label only when it differ
         +s from the                                                     
      13 +identifier (or contains characters not allowed in identifiers).
      14 +"""                                                            
      15 +                                                               
      16  from __future__ import annotations
      17  
      18 +import re                                                      
      19  from io import StringIO
      20  from pathlib import Path
      21  from typing import TYPE_CHECKING
     ...
       50

      274 +    # -- variable identifier allocation ----------------------
          +-----------------                                             
      275 +                                                              
      276 +    def _allocate_var_idents(self) -> None:                   
      277 +        """Assign each Variable in the program a unique identi
          +fier.                                                         
      278 +                                                              
      279 +        Algorithm:                                            
      280 +          - Sanitize the variable's label to a valid identifie
          +r (replace                                                    
      281 +            non-ident characters with underscore; ensure a lea
          +ding letter or                                                
      282 +            underscore).                                      
      283 +          - If the resulting identifier is already taken, appe
          +nd ``_2``,                                                    
      284 +            ``_3``, ... until unique.                         
      285 +          - Empty / non-identifier-friendly labels fall back t
          +o ``var``.                                                    
      286 +                                                              
      287 +        The mapping is keyed by ``Variable.id`` (the program-l
          +ocal integer ID)                                              
      288 +        so that two variables sharing the same Python label st
          +ill get distinct                                              
      289 +        identifiers.                                          
      290 +        """                                                   
      291 +        used: set[str] = set()                                
      292 +        for var in self._program.variables:                   
      293 +            base = _sanitize_to_ident(var.label) or "var"     
      294 +            ident = base                                      
      295 +            counter = 2                                       
      296 +            while ident in used:                              
      297 +                ident = f"{base}_{counter}"                   
      298 +                counter += 1                                  
      299 +            used.add(ident)                                   
      300 +            self._var_idents[var.id] = ident                  
      301 +                                                              
      302 +    # -- vendor collection -----------------------------------
          +-----------------                                             
      303 +                                                              
      304 +    @staticmethod                                             
      305 +    def _collect_vendors(block: Block) -> set[str]:           
      306 +        vendors: set[str] = set()                             
      307 +        for element in block.elements:                        
      308 +            if isinstance(element, Block):                    
      309 +                vendors.update(_Writer._collect_vendors(elemen
          +t))                                                           
      310 +            else:                                             
      311 +                vn = get_operation_vendor_name(type(element)) 
      312 +                if vn:                                        
      313 +                    vendors.add(vn[0])                        
      314 +        return vendors                                        
      315 +                                                              
      316 +                                                              
      317 +# ------------------------------------------------------------
          +---------------                                               
      318 +# Module-level helpers                                        
      319 +# ------------------------------------------------------------
          +---------------                                               
      320 +                                                              
      321 +                                                              
      322 +def _major_minor(version: str) -> str:                        
      323 +    """Truncate a semver string to ``major.minor`` (e.g. ``"0.
          +1.3" -> "0.1"``)."""                                          
      324 +    parts = version.split(".")                                
      325 +    if len(parts) < 2:                                        
      326 +        return f"{version}.0"                                 
      327 +    return f"{parts[0]}.{parts[1]}"                           
      328 +                                                              
      329 +                                                              
      330 +def _sanitize_to_ident(s: str) -> str:                        
      331 +    """Map an arbitrary string to a valid identifier-like form
          +.                                                             
      332 +                                                              
      333 +    Replaces every non-``[A-Za-z0-9_]`` character with ``_``. 
          +Prefixes a                                                    
      334 +    leading underscore if the first character is a digit. Retu
          +rns an empty                                                  
      335 +    string if ``s`` is empty (caller decides on a fallback).  
      336 +    """                                                       
      337 +    if not s:                                                 
      338 +        return ""                                             
      339 +    out = re.sub(r"[^A-Za-z0-9_]", "_", s)                    
      340 +    if out[0].isdigit():                                      
      341 +        out = "_" + out                                       
      342 +    return out                                                
      343 +                                                              
      344 +                                                              
      345 +def _escape_str(s: str) -> str:                               
      346 +    """Escape a string for embedding in double quotes inside a
          + ``.qp`` file."""                                             
      347 +    return s.replace("\\", "\\\\").replace('"', '\\"')

      231 +        """Parse a ``var <ident> ["<label>"]`` line.          
      232 +                                                              
      233 +        Returns ``(ident, label)``. If no label string is pres

      239 +            msg = f"`var` declaration requires an identifier: 
          +{line!r}"

      253 +            msg = f"too many tokens in `var` declaration: {lin
          +e!r}"                                                         
      254 +            raise ParseError(msg, self._pos + 1)              
      255 +        return ident, label

      417  def _uq(s: str) -> str:
      418      s = s.strip()
      419      if s.startswith('"') and s.endswith('"'):

      422  
      423  
      424 +def _unescape_str(s: str) -> str:                             
      425 +    """Inverse of the writer's escape: ``\\"`` -> ``"``, ``\\\
          +\`` -> ``\\``."""                                             
      426 +    out = []                                                  
      427 +    i = 0                                                     
      428 +    while i < len(s):                                         
      429 +        c = s[i]                                              
      430 +        if c == "\\" and i + 1 < len(s):                      
      431 +            nxt = s[i + 1]                                    
      432 +            if nxt == "\\":                                   
      433 +                out.append("\\")                              
      434 +                i += 2                                        
      435 +                continue                                      
      436 +            if nxt == '"':                                    
      437 +                out.append('"')                               
      438 +                i += 2                                        
      439 +                continue                                      
      440 +        out.append(c)                                         
      441 +        i += 1                                                
      442 +    return "".join(out)                                       
      443 +                                                              
      444 +

● All six tasks complete. Let me give you a summary of what changed and why.

  Summary

  The bug

  Variables were serialised by var.label, but labels are not unique:

  freq  = program.variable("freq")
  freq2 = program.variable("freq")  # same label, different Variable instance

  Both round-tripped to identical var freq lines and identical freq references
  in the body — so on load the two distinct variables collapsed into one, and
  the loops/operations that referenced them lost their bindings.

  Labels could also contain spaces and other non-identifier characters, but the
  file format had no way to quote them.

  Fix

  A clean separation between identifier (used to reference a variable in the
  file) and label (the free-form human-readable string):

  var freq                              # identifier=freq, label=freq
  var freq_2 "freq"                     # identifier=freq_2, label=freq
  (disambiguated)
  var drive_freq "Drive frequency"      # identifier=drive_freq, label="Drive
  frequency"
  var var ""                            # empty label falls back to identifier
  "var"
  var _1st_var "1st_var"                # leading digit gets underscore prefix

  When a label is omitted, identifier == label.

  Code changes

  File: serialization/writer.py
  Change: Rewritten as a _Writer class so per-program state (the variable
    identifier map) can be carried as instance attributes instead of threaded
    through every helper. New _allocate_var_idents() walks program.variables,
    sanitises each label to a valid identifier, and disambiguates collisions
  with
     _2, _3, …. The mapping is keyed by Variable.id so two variables sharing a
    Python label get distinct identifiers. New helpers: _sanitize_to_ident()
    (replace non-ident chars with _, prefix leading digits with _);
  _escape_str()
     (escape \ and " inside quoted labels).
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  File: serialization/parser.py
  Change: New _parse_var_decl() method tokenises var <ident> ["<label>"].
  Refused
    duplicate identifiers in the same scope with a clear ParseError. New
    _unescape_str() helper inverts the writer's escape. Also fixed a
  pre-existing
     bug in _construct_generic where false/true as a positional argument to a
    vendor operation was kept as the string "false"/"true" instead of being
    parsed to bool — now positional args go through _parse_arg like keyword
  args.

  Verified end-to-end

  ┌─────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │        Test         │                      Result                       │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Same-label          │ ✓ freq and freq_2 "freq" emitted; both round-trip │
  │ variables (the      │  to distinct Variable instances both labeled      │
  │ user's exact case)  │ "freq"; set_frequency operations bind to the      │
  │                     │ correct one; byte-for-byte stable round-trip      │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Labels with spaces  │ ✓ identifier Drive_frequency chosen, label        │
  │ ("Drive frequency") │ preserved verbatim                                │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Labels with         │                                                   │
  │ embedded quotes /   │ ✓ escaped via \" and \\, restored on parse        │
  │ backslashes         │                                                   │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Empty label         │ ✓ falls back to identifier var                    │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Label starting with │ ✓ identifier _1st_var                             │
  │  digit ("1st_var")  │                                                   │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Vendor op with bool │                                                   │

  │ (previous feature)  │ emitted/checked                                   │
  └─────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Notion .qp spec updated

  - Section 4.1 (Variable Declarations) rewritten:
    - Distinguishes "identifier" (parser anchor) from "label" (human-readable,
  optional, quoted string)
    - Identifier rules (Python-like): [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*
    - Concrete example showing two variables with the same label being
  disambiguated to freq and freq_2 "freq"
    - Notes that labels with \ or " are escaped
  - Section 7 (Grammar): var_decl := "var" IDENT [STRING]

  What changed

  Parser — _parse_var_decl() now requires exactly three tokens (var, identifier,
   quoted label). Anything else fails with one of two clear errors:
  - var declaration must have the form var <ident> "<label>". Got: 'var freq'
  - label after var freq must be a quoted string: 'var freq freq'

  Writer — already emits the canonical labelled form (from the previous step).
  No change needed.

  Notion .qp spec (Section 4.1) — removed the "hand-written shorthand" allowance
   and replaced it with a definitive statement:

  ▎ Every variable line has the form var <ident> "<label>" — the label is 
  ▎ mandatory, including when the identifier and label happen to be the same 
  ▎ string. […] The parser rejects all other forms (bare var freq, unquoted 
  ▎ labels, extra tokens) with a clear error — there is no shorthand.

  Grammar (Section 7) — var_decl := "var" IDENT STRING (no longer [STRING]).

  Verified

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │                     Form                      │         Result          │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ var freq "freq"                               │ accepted (canonical)    │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ var anon ""                                   │ accepted (empty label)  │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ var freq                                      │ rejected — must have    │
  │                                               │ "<label>"               │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ var freq freq                                 │ rejected — label must   │
  │                                               │ be quoted               │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ var freq "label" "extra"                      │ rejected — too many     │
  │                                               │ tokens                  │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Same-label vars round-trip (var freq "freq" + │ byte-stable             │
  │  var freq_2 "freq")                           │                         │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  Symmetric with the earlier require <vendor> <version> strict rule — version on
   require and label on var are both mandatory in the file format. No ambiguity,
   one canonical form, parser fails fast on malformed input.

✻ Sautéed for 1m 24s
